\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "Controlling Text-to-Image Diffusion by Orthogonal Finetuning"}

# Chapter 1: Introduction to Controlling Generative Models

The advent of large-scale text-to-image diffusion models represents a watershed moment in the history of computer graphics and artificial intelligence. These systems, such as Stable Diffusion, Imagen, and Glide, have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in synthesizing highly photorealistic images directly from textual descriptions. By leveraging massive datasets of paired text and image data alongside advanced architecture designs like Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) or Vector Quantized GANs to operate in compressed latent spaces, these models have moved beyond simple pattern recognition to genuine creative synthesis. They successfully navigate the complex distribution of visual data, allowing researchers and artists alike to translate abstract linguistic concepts into tangible visual forms with unprecedented fidelity. However, despite these impressive achievements, relying solely on text prompts reveals fundamental limitations when attempting to exert precise control over the generation process. The inherent ambiguity of natural language often proves insufficient for providing the fine-grained specifications required for specialized downstream tasks, creating a critical bottleneck for practical deployment in fields ranging from industrial design to medical imaging analysis.

To address the insufficiency of pure text guidance, the research community has pivoted toward developing methods that allow for external conditioning signals or subject-specific adaptations. Two primary domains have emerged to tackle these needs: subject-driven generation and controllable generation. Subject-driven generation focuses on personalization, aiming to teach a model about a specific object, animal, or concept using only a handful of reference images combined with a generic placeholder token. This allows users to place that unique entity into arbitrary contexts dictated by new text prompts. Conversely, controllable generation seeks to integrate auxiliary inputs—such as Canny edge detectors, semantic segmentation maps, or facial landmark coordinates—to dictate the spatial layout and structural properties of the generated image while adhering to a descriptive caption. Both tasks fundamentally require adapting large pretrained diffusion models without degrading their broad generative abilities established during initial training. The central academic challenge lies in striking a delicate equilibrium between training efficiency—the desire to minimize computational resources—and generalizability preservation—the imperative to retain the rich semantic knowledge encoded in the original model weights.

Existing strategies for addressing these challenges often rely on heuristic approaches that lack rigorous theoretical grounding. Common practices involve applying small learning rates to update neuron weights naively or introducing lightweight components through re-parameterization techniques like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). While intuitively appealing due to their simplicity, these methods offer no formal guarantees against catastrophic forgetting or the degradation of semantic coherence upon which high-quality generation depends. Furthermore, there exists a notable absence of principled mechanisms to quantify whether a finetuned model retains its original "knowledge" or has drifted into irrelevant noise representations. Current methodologies frequently assume that minimizing the Euclidean distance between updated weights and original weights equates to preserving functionality; however, this linear metric overlooks the intricate rotational symmetries and angular relationships that define neural network representations on high-dimensional manifolds. Consequently, there is an urgent need for a structured approach capable of explicitly modeling and preserving the internal geometry of neuronal interactions while enabling targeted behavioral modifications necessary for downstream adaptability.

The tension between adapting a model quickly enough for specific utility while keeping it robust enough for general application drives the motivation behind this chapter's exploration. We must acknowledge that brute-force updates often destabilize the delicate probability distributions learned by diffusion processes during pre-training attempts at precision control lead to outputs that look plausible superficially but fail structurally or semantically when scrutinized closely. Therefore, moving beyond ad-hoc adjustments requires deepening our understanding of *how* information is stored within neural layers—not merely in absolute magnitudes but specifically in directional orientations relative to one another on unit hyperspheres. Recognizing this distinction paves the way for innovative frameworks like Orthogonal Finetuning (OFT), which posits that altering neuron directions systematically offers greater efficacy than traditional scalar tweaks while ensuring structural integrity remains intact throughout optimisation cycles focused entirely on maximizing alignment with user intent versus default behaviors learned # Chapter 2: Foundations of Neural Network Geometry and Semantics

## Introduction to Geometric Principles in Fine-tuning Large Models

As we delve deeper into the realm of modern deep learning, particularly concerning massive text-to-image diffusion models, it becomes evident that successful application requires more than merely applying gradient descent updates to existing weights. The prevailing paradigm of fine-tuning relies heavily on heuristic assumptions about how changes in weight vectors correlate with the retention of semantic meaning. Before introducing the specific methodology known as Orthogonal Finetuning (OFT), it is essential to dismantle the limitations inherent in traditional fine-tuning strategies and establish a robust geometric foundation. This foundational perspective shifts our view from treating neural network weights as mere scalar values accumulating numerical adjustments to viewing them as directional entities residing on complex manifolds. The core insight driving advanced finetuning techniques is that the relative orientation—specifically the angles between neurons—is paramount for encoding semantic knowledge, while absolute magnitudes serve a subsidiary role. Understanding this distinction is critical for grasping why standard approaches often fail to maintain generative capability during adaptation and how newer geometric constraints offer a principled solution.

Traditional fine-tuning methodologies operate under a tacit assumption that keeping the updated model close to the pretrained model in Euclidean space ensures the preservation of functionality. This philosophy manifests practically through the adoption of extremely small learning rates or low-rank adaptations like LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation). The rationale behind limiting step sizes or restricting parameter dimensions is to induce a "perturbation" rather than a fundamental restructuring of the network's internal logic. Mathematically, practitioners attempt to minimize the Frobenius norm, denoted as $\| \bm{M} - \bm{M}^0 \|$, where $\bm{M}$ represents the weights after fine-tuning and $\bm{M}^0$ represents the pretrained weights. The belief is straightforward: if the distance between old and new weights is negligible, then the function performed by the network should remain nearly identical, thereby retaining its pre-trained generalization abilities while acquiring slight modifications for downstream tasks. However, as explored in recent literature addressing this exact issue, relying solely on Euclidean proximity is fundamentally flawed. It ignores the topological arrangement of neurons and presupposes that linear interpolation in weight space corresponds intuitively to functional continuity in probability space. Consequently, many attempts at aggressive fine-tuning result in catastrophic forgetting or mode collapse, precisely because they ignore the geometric integrity required to sustain complex generative behaviors found in diffusion models.

To rectify this oversight, we must shift our analytical lens toward spherical geometry. Instead of focusing on whether two weight matrices are numerically similar, we should investigate whether they possess similar internal relational structures. This transition begins with normalizing every individual neuron (weight vector) onto a unit hypersphere. Once normalized, each neuron becomes a point on a surface where position is determined entirely by direction rather than length. This normalization process reveals that neural networks encode vast amounts of information through the interplay between these directional components—their angles relative to one another—and their collective distribution across this high-dimensional sphere. By abstracting away magnitude initially, we isolate pure angular relationships, allowing us to define quantities such as hyperspherical energy, which measures how densely packed or spread out these neurons are on their respective spheres. This conceptual leap sets the stage for understanding why preserving these specific relational properties allows for safe adaptation without destroying previously acquired knowledge bases embedded within millions of parameters. Furthermore, recognizing that rotations preserve distances facilitates discussions about orthogonal transformations later on; thus establishing early definitions here paves roads toward rigorous proofs involving stability and convergence characteristics necessary for practical deployment scenarios today [reference: Liu et al., 2021]. Ultimately mastering these principles enables researchers design algorithms capable manipulating entire distributions systematically ensuring optimal outcomes regardless subsequent operational # Chapter 3: Understanding Hyperspherical Energy as a Stability Metric

When attempting to adapt massive text-to-image diffusion models like Stable Diffusion for specific downstream tasks, researchers face a fundamental dilemma: how to teach the model something new without unlearning what it already knows about the world. Traditional fine-tuning strategies rely on heuristic assumptions, often assuming that keeping the total squared difference between old and new weights small ensures stability. However, as highlighted in recent literature, this view misses a critical nuance inherent to how neural networks encode meaning. Central to the proposed Orthogonal Fine-tuning (OFT) methodology is a deeper, more robust mathematical concept known as **Hyperspherical Energy** (HE). This chapter explores this concept in detail, moving beyond surface-level definitions to build an intuitive understanding of why maintaining this specific energetic configuration of neurons is paramount for preserving the semantic integrity of large-scale generative models.

To grasp Hyperspherical Energy, one must first appreciate the geometry of the neuron representations themselves. Neural networks operate in high-dimensional spaces where individual neurons are vectors pointing in various directions. Crucially, many modern analyses suggest that the *magnitude* (length) of these vectors carries less semantic significance than their *orientation*. Imagine standing in a vast room filled with people facing every conceivable direction; who you interact with depends primarily on whether they are looking toward you or away from you, not necessarily on their height or stride length. In machine learning theory, specifically referencing works cited by Liu et al., it has been observed that normalizing all neurons to lie on a unit hypersphere captures the majority of semantic information. Consequently, before defining energy itself, we must rigorously define how we measure the relationship between any two points on this sphere.

### Definition of Pairwise Neuron Similarity (Cosine/Similarity)

At the heart of measuring neuronal interactions is the notion of pairwise similarity. Consider a single layer within a neural network containing \(n\) neurons. Let us denote the weight vector associated with the \(i\)-th neuron as \(\bm{w}_i \in \mathbb{R}^d\), where \(d\) represents the dimensionality of the inputs fed into that layer. The raw values within this vector determine both how strongly a particular feature activates and in which abstract direction that feature lies within the input space. However, to isolate purely directional information—the aspect most responsible for encoding complex patterns—we normalize these vectors to create unit vectors. We define \(\hat{\bm{w}}_i = \bm{w}_i / \|\bm{w}_i\|\), ensuring that every point resides precisely on the surface of a unit hypersphere embedded in \(\mathbb{R}^d\).

Once normalized, determining how related two neurons are becomes a matter of calculating their relative orientation. The primary tool for this calculation is cosine similarity. Mathematically expressed as \(\cos(\theta) = \langle \hat{\bm{w}}_i, \hat{\bm{w}}_j \rangle = \hat{\bm{w}}_i^\top \hat{\bm{w}}_j\), this value ranges from -1 to 1. A result close to 1 indicates that two neurons are highly correlated and activate similarly for overlapping sets of inputs; they point in nearly identical directions. Conversely, a value near -1 signifies opposing behaviors, while 0 implies orthogonality or independence between features captured by those neurons. While standard correlation tells us about alignment directly through dot products, analyzing spatial distribution requires translating this angular relationship into distances along the curved surface of the sphere or straight-line chord lengths in Euclidean space connecting them on that sphere's diameter range [this needs clarification]. Actually, simpler geometric intuition relies on Euclidean distance between endpoints on the sphere boundary versus spherical arc distance; however, let us proceed strictly with the definition provided by Huang et al.'s derivation context found here: utilizing reciprocal Euclidean distances between these endpoint locations serves as a proxy for interaction intensity where closer proximity corresponds to higher energy density per pair if modeled physically repelling particles would lower it... wait no..

Let's refine: Cosine similarity relates inversely to Euclidean distance between normalized vectors because if they align closely (angle ~0), their linear separation is minimal (\(| | = 0\)), whereas divergence increases separation until maximum at opposite ends (\(|| |=2\)). So smaller physical distance equals greater functional overlap/similarity; conversely larger gaps equal dissimilarities requiring independent pathways within hidden dimensions thus reducing interference risks etcetera.. Now combining pairs across entire layer yields aggregate statistic called hyperenergy!
